    29	}
    30	
    31	const cachedSet = new Set<Entity>();
    32	const cachedQueue = [] as Entity[];
    33	
    34	export function destroyEntity(world: World, entity: Entity) {
    35	    const ctx = world[$internal];
    36	
    37	    // Check if entity exists.
    38	    if (!world.has(entity)) throw new Error('Koota: The entity being destroyed does not exist.');
    39	
    40	    // Caching the lookup in the outer scope of the loop increases performance.
    41	    const entityQueue = cachedQueue;
    42	    const processedEntities = cachedSet;
    43	
    44	    // Ensure the queue is empty before starting.
    45	    entityQueue.length = 0;
    46	    entityQueue.push(entity);
    47	    processedEntities.clear();
    48	
    49	    // Destroyed entities may be the target or source of relations.
    50	    // To avoid stale references, all these relations must be removed.
    51	    // autoDestroy controls cascade behavior:
    52	    // - 'source' (or 'orphan'): when target dies, destroy sources (e.g., parent dies → children die)
    53	    // - 'target': when source dies, destroy targets (e.g., container dies → items die)
    54	    while (entityQueue.length > 0) {
    55	        const currentEntity = entityQueue.pop()!;
    56	        if (processedEntities.has(currentEntity)) continue;
    57	
    58	        processedEntities.add(currentEntity);
    59	
    60	        for (const relation of ctx.relations) {
    61	            const relationCtx = relation[$internal];
    62	
    63	            // Handle entities that have relations pointing TO currentEntity (currentEntity is target)
    64	            // If autoDestroy is 'orphan', destroy those sources
    65	            const sources = getEntitiesWithRelationTo(world, relation, currentEntity);
    66	            for (const source of sources) {
    67	                if (!world.has(source)) continue;
    68	
    69	                // Remove the relation from source to currentEntity
    70	                cleanupRelationTarget(world, relation, source, currentEntity);
    71	
    72	                // If autoDestroy: 'source', queue the source for destruction
    73	                if (relationCtx.autoDestroy === 'source') entityQueue.push(source);
    74	            }
    75	
    76	            // Handle relations where currentEntity is the source pointing to targets
    77	            // If autoDestroy is 'target', destroy those targets
    78	            if (relationCtx.autoDestroy === 'target') {
    79	                const targets = getRelationTargets(world, relation, currentEntity);
    80	                for (const target of targets) {
    81	                    if (!world.has(target)) continue;
    82	                    if (!processedEntities.has(target)) entityQueue.push(target);
    83	                }
    84	            }
    85	        }
    86	
    87	        // Remove all traits of the current entity.
    88	        const entityTraits = ctx.entityTraits.get(currentEntity);
    89	        if (entityTraits) {
    90	            for (const trait of entityTraits) {
    91	                removeTrait(world, currentEntity, trait);
    92	            }
    93	        }
    94	
    95	        // Free the entity.
    96	        releaseEntity(ctx.entityIndex, currentEntity);
    97	
    98	        // Remove the entity from the all query.
    99	        const allQuery = ctx.queriesHashMap.get('');
   100	        if (allQuery) allQuery.remove(world, currentEntity);
   101	
   102	        // Remove all entity state from world.
   103	        ctx.entityTraits.delete(currentEntity);
   104	
   105	        // Clear entity bitmasks.
   106	        const eid = getEntityId(currentEntity);
   107	        for (let i = 0; i < ctx.entityMasks.length; i++) {
   108	            ctx.entityMasks[i][eid] = 0;
   109	        }
   110	    }
   111	}

   176	/**
   177	 * Add a relation pair to an entity.
   178	 */
   179	/* @inline */ function addRelationPair(world: World, entity: Entity, pair: RelationPair) {
   180	    const pairCtx = pair[$internal];
   181	    const relation = pairCtx.relation;
   182	    const target = pairCtx.target;
   183	
   184	    // Only specific targets can be added (not wildcard '*')
   185	    if (typeof target !== 'number') return;
   186	
   187	    const params = pairCtx.params;
   188	    const relationCtx = relation[$internal];
   189	    const relationTrait = relationCtx.trait;
   190	
   191	    // Ignore if entity already relates to this target
   192	    // For example, adding Likes(alice) when this pair is already on the entity.
   193	    if (hasRelationToTarget(world, relation, entity, target)) return;
   194	
   195	    // For exclusive relations, remove the old target first
   196	    if (relationCtx.exclusive) {
   197	        const oldTarget = getFirstRelationTarget(world, relation, entity);
   198	        if (oldTarget !== undefined && oldTarget !== target) {
   199	            const instance = getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relationTrait);
   200	            if (instance) {
   201	                for (const sub of instance.removeSubscriptions) sub(entity, oldTarget);
   202	            }
   203	            removeRelationTarget(world, relation, entity, oldTarget);
   204	        }
   205	    }
   206	
   207	    let instance = addTraitToEntity(world, entity, relationTrait);
   208	
   209	    const targetIndex = addRelationTarget(world, relation, entity, target);
   210	    if (targetIndex === -1) return; // No-op
   211	
   212	    const schema =
   213	        instance?.schema ?? getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relationTrait)!.schema;
   214	    const defaults = getSchemaDefaults(schema, relationTrait[$internal].type);
   215	
   216	    if (defaults) {
   217	        setRelationDataAtIndex(world, entity, relation, targetIndex, { ...defaults, ...params });
   218	    } else if (params) {
   219	        setRelationDataAtIndex(world, entity, relation, targetIndex, params);
   220	    }
   221	
   222	    // Fire add subscription for this pair
   223	    instance = instance ?? getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relationTrait)!;
   224	    for (const sub of instance.addSubscriptions) sub(entity, target);
   225	}
   226	
   227	export function removeTrait(world: World, entity: Entity, ...traits: (Trait | RelationPair)[]) {
   228	    for (let i = 0; i < traits.length; i++) {
   229	        const trait = traits[i];
   230	
   231	        if (isRelationPair(trait)) {
   232	            removeRelationPair(world, entity, trait);
   233	            continue;
   234	        }
   235	
   236	        if (!hasTrait(world, entity, trait)) continue;
   237	
   238	        const traitCtx = trait[$internal];
   239	
   240	        if (traitCtx.relation) {
   241	            // Relation trait: emit per-pair removes, then teardown
   242	            const instance = getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, trait);
   243	            if (instance) {
   244	                const targets = getRelationTargets(world, traitCtx.relation, entity);
   245	                for (const t of targets) {
   246	                    for (const sub of instance.removeSubscriptions) sub(entity, t);
   247	                }
   248	            }
   249	            removeAllRelationTargets(world, traitCtx.relation, entity);
   250	        } else {
   251	            // Regular trait: emit generic remove
   252	            const instance = getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, trait);
   253	            if (instance) {
   254	                for (const sub of instance.removeSubscriptions) sub(entity);
   255	            }
   256	        }
   257	
   258	        removeTraitFromEntity(world, entity, trait);
   259	    }

   319	export function hasTrait(world: World, entity: Entity, trait: Trait): boolean {
   320	    const ctx = world[$internal];
   321	    const instance = getTraitInstance(ctx.traitInstances, trait);
   322	    if (!instance) return false;
   323	
   324	    const { generationId, bitflag } = instance;
   325	    const eid = getEntityId(entity);
   326	    const mask = ctx.entityMasks[generationId][eid];
   327	
   328	    return (mask & bitflag) === bitflag;
   329	}

   351	export function getTrait(world: World, entity: Entity, trait: Trait | RelationPair) {

   424	/**
   425	 * Core logic for adding a trait to an entity.

   427	/* @inline */ function addTraitToEntity(
   428	    world: World,
   429	    entity: Entity,
   430	    trait: Trait
   431	): TraitInstance | undefined {
   432	    // Exit early if the entity already has the trait
   433	    if (hasTrait(world, entity, trait)) return undefined;
   434	
   435	    const ctx = world[$internal];
   436	
   437	    // Register the trait if it's not already registered
   438	    if (!hasTraitInstance(ctx.traitInstances, trait)) registerTrait(world, trait);
   439	
   440	    const instance = getTraitInstance(ctx.traitInstances, trait)!;
   441	    const { generationId, bitflag, queries, trackingQueries } = instance;
   442	
   443	    // Add bitflag to entity bitmask
   444	    const eid = getEntityId(entity);
   445	    ctx.entityMasks[generationId][eid] |= bitflag;
   446	
   447	    // Set the entity as dirty
   448	    for (const dirtyMask of ctx.dirtyMasks.values()) {
   449	        if (!dirtyMask[generationId]) dirtyMask[generationId] = [];
   450	        dirtyMask[generationId][eid] |= bitflag;
   451	    }
   452	
   453	    // Update non-tracking queries (no event data needed)
   454	    for (const query of queries) {
   455	        query.toRemove.remove(entity);
   456	        // Use checkQueryWithRelations if query has relation filters, otherwise use checkQuery
   457	        const match =
   458	            query.relationFilters && query.relationFilters.length > 0
   459	                ? checkQueryWithRelations(world, query, entity)
   460	                : query.check(world, entity);
   461	        if (match) query.add(entity);
   462	        else query.remove(world, entity);
   463	    }
   464	
   465	    // Update tracking queries (with event data)
   466	    for (const query of trackingQueries) {
   467	        query.toRemove.remove(entity);
   468	        // Use checkQueryTrackingWithRelations if query has relation filters, otherwise use checkQueryTracking
   469	        const match =
   470	            query.relationFilters && query.relationFilters.length > 0
   471	                ? checkQueryTrackingWithRelations(world, query, entity, 'add', generationId, bitflag)
   472	                : query.checkTracking(world, entity, 'add', generationId, bitflag);
   473	        if (match) query.add(entity);
   474	        else query.remove(world, entity);
   475	    }
   476	
   477	    // Add trait to entity internally
   478	    ctx.entityTraits.get(entity)!.add(trait);
   479	
   480	    return instance;
   481	}

import type { Relation, RelationPair } from '../relation/types';
import type {
    ConfigurableTrait,
    ExtractSchema,
    SetTraitCallback,
    Trait,
    TraitRecord,
    TraitValue,
} from '../trait/types';

export type Entity = number & {
    add: (...traits: ConfigurableTrait[]) => void;
    remove: (...traits: (Trait | RelationPair)[]) => void;
    has: (trait: Trait | RelationPair) => boolean;
    destroy: () => void;
    changed: (trait: Trait) => void;
    set: <T extends Trait | RelationPair>(
        trait: T,
        value: TraitValue<ExtractSchema<T>> | SetTraitCallback<T>,
        flagChanged?: boolean
    ) => void;
    get: <T extends Trait | RelationPair>(trait: T) => TraitRecord<ExtractSchema<T>> | undefined;
    targetFor: <T extends Trait>(relation: Relation<T>) => Entity | undefined;
    targetsFor: <T extends Trait>(relation: Relation<T>) => Entity[];
    id: () => number;
    generation: () => number;
    isAlive: () => boolean;
};

import { Brand } from '../../common';
import { Trait } from '../../trait/types';
import { Relation, RelationPair } from '../types';
import { $relation, $relationPair } from '../symbols';

/**
 * Check if a value is a Relation
 */
export /* @inline @pure */ function isRelation(value: unknown): value is Relation<Trait> {
    return (value as Brand<typeof $relation> | null | undefined)?.[$relation] as unknown as boolean;
}

/**
 * Check if a value is a RelationPair
 */
export /* @inline @pure */ function isRelationPair(value: unknown): value is RelationPair {
    return (value as Brand<typeof $relationPair> | null | undefined)?.[
        $relationPair
    ] as unknown as boolean;
}

   132	export function addTrait(world: World, entity: Entity, ...traits: ConfigurableTrait[]) {
   133	    for (let i = 0; i < traits.length; i++) {
   134	        const config = traits[i];
   135	
   136	        // Handle relation pairs
   137	        if (isRelationPair(config)) {
   138	            addRelationPair(world, entity, config);
   139	            continue;
   140	        }
   141	
   142	        // Get trait and params for regular traits
   143	        let trait: Trait;
   144	        let params: Record<string, any> | undefined;
   145	
   146	        if (Array.isArray(config)) {
   147	            [trait, params] = config as [Trait, Record<string, any>];
   148	        } else {
   149	            trait = config as Trait;
   150	        }
   151	
   152	        // Add the trait to the entity
   153	        const data = addTraitToEntity(world, entity, trait);
   154	        if (!data) continue; // Already had the trait
   155	
   156	        // Initialize values
   157	        const traitCtx = trait[$internal];
   158	
   159	        const defaults = isOrderedTrait(trait)
   160	            ? getOrderedTrait(world, entity, trait)
   161	            : getSchemaDefaults(data.schema, traitCtx.type);
   162	
   163	        if (traitCtx.type === 'aos') {
   164	            setTrait(world, entity, trait, params ?? defaults, false);
   165	        } else if (defaults) {
   166	            setTrait(world, entity, trait, { ...defaults, ...params }, false);
   167	        } else if (params) {
   168	            setTrait(world, entity, trait, params, false);
   169	        }
   170	
   171	        // Call add subscriptions after values are set
   172	        for (const sub of data.addSubscriptions) sub(entity);
   173	    }
   174	}

   177	 * Add a relation pair to an entity.

   179	/* @inline */ function addRelationPair(world: World, entity: Entity, pair: RelationPair) {
   180	    const pairCtx = pair[$internal];
   181	    const relation = pairCtx.relation;
   182	    const target = pairCtx.target;
   183	
   184	    // Only specific targets can be added (not wildcard '*')
   185	    if (typeof target !== 'number') return;
   186	
   187	    const params = pairCtx.params;
   188	    const relationCtx = relation[$internal];
   189	    const relationTrait = relationCtx.trait;
   190	
   191	    // Ignore if entity already relates to this target
   192	    // For example, adding Likes(alice) when this pair is already on the entity.
   193	    if (hasRelationToTarget(world, relation, entity, target)) return;
   194	
   195	    // For exclusive relations, remove the old target first
   196	    if (relationCtx.exclusive) {
   197	        const oldTarget = getFirstRelationTarget(world, relation, entity);
   198	        if (oldTarget !== undefined && oldTarget !== target) {
   199	            const instance = getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relationTrait);
   200	            if (instance) {
   201	                for (const sub of instance.removeSubscriptions) sub(entity, oldTarget);
   202	            }
   203	            removeRelationTarget(world, relation, entity, oldTarget);
   204	        }
   205	    }
   206	
   207	    let instance = addTraitToEntity(world, entity, relationTrait);
   208	
   209	    const targetIndex = addRelationTarget(world, relation, entity, target);
   210	    if (targetIndex === -1) return; // No-op
   211	
   212	    const schema =
   213	        instance?.schema ?? getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relationTrait)!.schema;
   214	    const defaults = getSchemaDefaults(schema, relationTrait[$internal].type);
   215	
   216	    if (defaults) {
   217	        setRelationDataAtIndex(world, entity, relation, targetIndex, { ...defaults, ...params });
   218	    } else if (params) {
   219	        setRelationDataAtIndex(world, entity, relation, targetIndex, params);
   220	    }
   221	
   222	    // Fire add subscription for this pair
   223	    instance = instance ?? getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relationTrait)!;
   224	    for (const sub of instance.addSubscriptions) sub(entity, target);
   225	}

REASONING_CONTENT
Now I understand the core architecture. Let me understand what the task is asking:

"Tracking modifiers detect trait-level additions and removals but cannot distinguish which specific relation pair changed, blocking per-target
reactivity."

So currently, `Added(ChildOf)` will track an entity when any relation pair is added/removed, but you can't track `Added(ChildOf(parent))`
specifically, i.e., the tracking modifier factory should accept a `RelationPair` argument, and then the modifier should distinguish which specific
relation pair changed.

The task requirements:

1. **Make tracking modifier factories accept new `RelationPair`.** The target `'*'` acts as a wildcard.

   So `Added`, `Removed`, `Changed` factories should accept RelationPair (e.g., `Added(Likes(alice))`, `Added(Likes('*'))`). Currently `createAdded`
only accepts `TraitOrRelation[]` and maps relations to their underlying trait. It should also accept RelationPair.

2. **Non-first pair additions and non-last pair removals are detected at pair level.** This is interesting. The current relation target
additions/removals use the trait bitmask at the base trait level. For `Added(ChildOf)`:
   - Adding the first ChildOf pair causes `addTraitToEntity` to fire (trait-level add).
   - Adding a second ChildOf pair (entity already has ChildOf trait) does NOT cause a trait-level add event, so `Added(ChildOf)` won't detect it.

   For pair-level detection, when a relation pair is added/removed, we need to fire tracking at the pair level. "Non-first pair additions and non-last
pair removals are detected at pair level" means:
   - First pair addition: trait is added → both trait-level and pair-level detection (or trait-level enough).
   - Non-first pair addition (adding another target when the entity already has the trait): must be detected at pair level because trait-level add
doesn't fire.
   - Non-last pair removal (removing one target when the entity still has other targets): must be detected at pair level because trait-level remove
doesn't fire.
   - Last pair removal: trait removed → trait-level detection.

3. **Exclusive replacement produces both a removal and an addition.** For exclusive relation `Targeting`, replacing target A with B should produce
pair-level removal of `Targeting(A)` and pair-level addition of `Targeting(B)`.

4. **Modifier factories are long-lived and reused across world resets.** The tracking masks (`setTrackingMasks`) snapshot is created when factory
created, for existing worlds. "Across world resets" - there might be a world reset mechanism. Need to look at universe/world resets. The modifiers
should continue to work after a world reset (new world created with same entity masks?).

5. **Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel.** So if you add `Likes(alice)` then remove `Likes(alice)` within
the same window (before query is run), then neither `Added(Likes(alice))` nor `Removed(Likes(alice))` should match. This is similar to how trait-level
dirty masks work? Actually currently for traits, add then remove within window results in both add and remove? Let me check. For a trait:
`addTraitToEntity` sets dirty bit, then `removeTraitFromEntity` sets dirty bit again. The snapshot comparison: added if oldMask has no bit and current
has bit → not added if removed. Removed if oldMask has bit and current has 0 → but if it was added (old has no bit), then removed → both old and
current have no bit, so the removed check also checks dirtyMask to catch remove after add. So for trait-level, add-then-remove would match Removed
(via dirty) but not Added. That's not canceling. But the task says "Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel" - so
for pair-level, they want add + remove to cancel out, i.e., neither Added nor Removed triggers for that target.

6. **Entity destruction fires pair-level removal for all active pairs.** When an entity is destroyed, all its relation pairs are removed. Currently
`destroyEntity` calls `removeTrait` which calls `removeRelationPair`-like path that emits remove subscriptions per pair and `removeTraitFromEntity`
fires tracking at trait level. But with pair tracking, destruction should fire pair-level removal for each active pair so `Removed(Likes(alice))`
matches.

7. **Pair modifiers compose with `Or`.** `Or(Added(Likes(alice)), Added(Other(...)))` etc.

8. **Different pair targets produce distinct cached queries.** The query hash must distinguish different targets. It already does for relation pairs
(relationId * 10000000 + targetId). But the modifier currently maps relation pairs to base trait, losing target info. Need to keep the pair info in
the modifier.

9. **Pair modifiers combined with regular trait parameters in the same query must satisfy all constraints together.** E.g.,
`world.query(Added(Likes(alice)), Position)` should only return entities that both added Likes(alice) AND have Position.

10. **The `entity.changed` method accepts a `RelationPair` for manual pair-level change signaling.** Currently `changed` only accepts Trait and calls
`setChanged`. Should accept RelationPair and call `setPairChanged`.

11. **Query result iteration resolves per-target relation data for pair-tracked traits.** Currently `getQueryStores` for a modifier just uses
`param.traits` (base traits) and gets the full trait store. For a pair-tracked relation trait with a store, iterating `query.readEach(([data]) =>
...)` should resolve per-target relation data. This is important: if you have `Added(Likes(alice))` with `Likes` having a store, then `readEach`
should give `{ amount: 100 }` for that entity's `Likes(alice)` data, not the full `Likes` data. Actually currently for a relation pair param,
`getQueryStores` just pushes baseTrait + store; and `getTrait`/snapshot would use the base trait's get which for non-exclusive relations returns...
let me check what `ctx.get` for a relation trait does. The base trait is a regular SoA/AoS trait. For a relation with store, the store is an array per
target? Let me examine storage. Actually relation targets store data via `setRelationDataAtIndex` which for SoA stores arrays per target index in each
field's store array. The base trait's `ctx.get(eid, store)` returns `{ amount: [100, 50] }` or something. So per-target data needs resolution.

Let me now understand the architecture more, then design.

    30	export function createWorld(

    34	    const id = allocateWorldId(universe.worldIndex);
    35	    let isInitialized = false;
    36	    let lazyTraits: ConfigurableTrait[] | undefined;
    37	    type HookInput = Trait | Relation<Trait> | RelationPair<Trait>;
    38	    type HookCallback = (entity: Entity, target?: Entity) => void;
    39	
    40	    function resolveHookTrait(input: HookInput): Trait {
    41	        if (isRelationPair(input)) return input[$internal].relation[$internal].trait;
    42	        if (isRelation(input)) return input[$internal].trait;
    43	        return input;
    44	    }
    45	
    46	    function resolveHookCallback(input: HookInput, callback: HookCallback): HookCallback {
    47	        if (isRelationPair(input)) {
    48	            const pairTarget = input[$internal].target;
    49	            if (pairTarget === '*') return callback;
    50	            return (entity: Entity, target?: Entity) => {
    51	                if (target === pairTarget) callback(entity, target);
    52	            };
    53	        }
    54	        return callback;
    55	    }
    56	
    57	    const world = {

    59	            entityIndex: createEntityIndex(id),
    60	            entityMasks: [[]],
    61	            entityTraits: new Map(),

    80	        init(...initTraits: ConfigurableTrait[]) {

    82	            if (isInitialized) return;
    83	
    84	            isInitialized = true;
    85	            universe.worlds[id] = world;
    86	
    87	            // Create uninitialized added masks.

    93	            // Register system traits.

   100	                lazyTraits = undefined;
   101	            }

   128	        set<T extends Trait>(trait: T, value: TraitValue<ExtractSchema<T>> | SetTraitCallback<T>) {
   129	            setTrait(world, world[$internal].worldEntity, trait, value, true);

   131	
   132	        destroy() {
   133	            // Destroy world entity.

   135	            world[$internal].worldEntity = null!;

   140	            releaseWorldId(universe.worldIndex, id);
   141	            universe.worlds[id] = null;
   142	        },
   143	
   144	        reset() {
   145	            lazyTraits = undefined;
   146	            const ctx = world[$internal];
   147	
   148	            // Destroy all entities so any cleanup is done.
   149	            world.entities.forEach((entity) => {
   150	                // Some relations may have caused the entity to be destroyed before
   151	                // we get to them in the loop.
   152	                if (world.has(entity)) {
   153	                    destroyEntity(world, entity);
   154	                }
   155	            });
   156	
   157	            ctx.entityIndex = createEntityIndex(id);
   158	            ctx.entityTraits.clear();
   159	            ctx.entityMasks = [[]];
   160	            ctx.bitflag = 1;
   161	
   162	            clearTraitInstance(ctx.traitInstances);
   163	            world.traits.clear();
   164	            ctx.relations.clear();
   165	
   166	            ctx.queriesHashMap.clear();
   167	            ctx.queryInstances.length = 0;
   168	            ctx.actionInstances.length = 0;
   169	            ctx.dirtyQueries.clear();

STEP 30 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
Important: The world `reset()` clears `trackingSnapshots`, `dirtyMasks`, `changedMasks` but does NOT re-run `setTrackingMasks` for existing tracking
modifier IDs! This is the "Modifier factories are long-lived and reused across world resets" issue. Currently after `world.reset()`, tracking masks
are cleared, so tracking modifiers created before reset no longer have snapshots. Actually wait, in `reset()` they call
`ctx.trackingSnapshots.clear()`, etc. Then `createEntity(world, IsExcluded)` is called, which registers IsExcluded but does not re-create masks for
existing modifier IDs. So `Added` queries after reset would break (they'd find `ctx.trackingSnapshots.get(id)` undefined). So the task wants us to fix
this: modifier factories are long-lived and reused across world resets. This means we need to re-initialize tracking masks for all created modifier
IDs on world reset. Also universe.reset may need to handle it.

Let me check the tests to understand expected behavior and current test setup.

import { beforeEach, describe, expect, it } from 'vitest';
import {
    $internal,
    createAdded,
    createChanged,
    createRemoved,
    createWorld,
    getStore,
    Not,
    Or,
    relation,
    trait,
} from '../src';

const Position = trait({ x: 0, y: 0 });
const IsActive = trait();
const Foo = trait();
const Bar = trait();

describe('Query modifiers', () => {
    const world = createWorld();
    world.init();

    beforeEach(() => {
        world.reset();
    });

    it('should correctly populate Not queries when traits are added and removed', () => {
        const entityA = world.spawn();
        const entityB = world.spawn();
        const entityC = world.spawn();

        let entities: any = world.query(Foo);
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);
        expect(entities[1]).toBe(entityB);
        expect(entities[2]).toBe(entityC);

        // Add
        entityA.add(Foo);
        entityB.add(Bar);
        entityC.add(Foo, Bar);

        entities = world.query(Foo);
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);
        expect(entities[1]).toBe(entityC);

        entities = world.query(Foo, Bar);
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityC);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityB);

        // Remove
        entityA.remove(Foo);

        entities = world.query(Foo);
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityC);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityB);
        expect(entities[1]).toBe(entityA);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo), Not(Bar));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);

        // Remove more so entity A and C have no traits
        entityC.remove(Foo);
        entityC.remove(Bar);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo), Not(Bar));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(2);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(3);
    });

    it('modifiers can be added as one call or separately', () => {
        const ctx = world[$internal];
        const entity = world.spawn();
        entity.add(Position, IsActive);

        let entities: any = world.query(Not(Foo), Not(Bar));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(1);

        entities = world.query(Not(Foo, Bar));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(1);

        // These queries should be hashed the same.
        expect(ctx.queriesHashMap.size).toBe(1);
    });

    it('should correctly populate Added queries when traits are added', () => {
        const Added = createAdded();

        const entityA = world.spawn();
        const entityB = world.spawn();
        const entityC = world.spawn();

        let entities: readonly number[] = [];

        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0);

        entityA.add(Foo);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);

        // The query gets drained and should be empty when run again.
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0);

        entityB.add(Foo);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityB);

        // And a static query should give both entities.
        entities = world.query(Foo);
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);
        expect(entities[1]).toBe(entityB);

        // Should not be added to the query if the trait is removed before it is read.
        entityC.add(Foo);
        entityC.remove(Foo);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0);

        // But if it is removed and added again in the same frame it should be recorded.
        entityA.remove(Foo);
        entityA.add(Foo);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);

        // Should only populate the query if tracked trait is added,
        // even if it matches the query otherwise.
        entityA.remove(Foo, Bar); // Quick reset
        entityA.add(Foo);
        world.query(Added(Foo)); // Drain query

        entityA.add(Bar);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0); // Fails for Added
        entities = world.query(Foo, Bar);
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA); // But matches static query
    });

    it('should properly populate Added queries with mulitple tracked traits', () => {
        const Added = createAdded();

        const entityA = world.spawn();
        const entityB = world.spawn();

        let entities = world.query(Added(Foo, Bar));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0);

        entityA.add(Foo);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo, Bar));
        expect(entities.length).toBe(0);

        entityA.add(Bar);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo, Bar));
        expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);

        entityB.add(Foo);
        entities = world.query(Added(Foo, Bar));

        entityC.remove(Foo);

        expect(entities).toContain(entityC);
        expect(entities.length).toBe(1);
    });

        const ChildOf = relation({ store: { order: 0 } });
        const Changed = createChanged();

        const parentA = world.spawn();
        const parentB = world.spawn();
        const childA = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentA));
        const childB = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentB));

        // No changes yet
        expect(world.query(Changed(ChildOf))).toHaveLength(0);

        // Change only childA
        childA.set(ChildOf(parentA), { order: 1 });
        let changed = world.query(Changed(ChildOf));
        expect(changed).toHaveLength(1);
        expect(changed).toContain(childA);

        // Change both, query filtered by parentA pair
        childA.set(ChildOf(parentA), { order: 2 });
        childB.set(ChildOf(parentB), { order: 3 });
        const filteredA = world.query(Changed(ChildOf), ChildOf(parentA));
        expect(filteredA).toHaveLength(1);
        expect(filteredA).toContain(childA);
    });

    it('should track Added on a relation', () => {
        const ChildOf = relation();
        const Added = createAdded();

        const parentA = world.spawn();
        const parentB = world.spawn();

        const childA = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentA));
        const childB = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentB));
        const childC = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentA));

        // Filtered by parentA: only childA and childC target parentA
        const filteredA = world.query(Added(ChildOf), ChildOf(parentA));
        expect(filteredA).toHaveLength(2);
        expect(filteredA).toContain(childA);
        expect(filteredA).toContain(childC);
        expect(filteredA).not.toContain(childB);
    });

    it('should track Removed on a relation', () => {
        const ChildOf = relation();
        const Removed = createRemoved();

        const parentA = world.spawn();
        const parentB = world.spawn();
        const childA = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentA));
        const childB = world.spawn(ChildOf(parentB));

        // No removals yet
        expect(world.query(Removed(ChildOf))).toHaveLength(0);

        // Remove childA's relation
        childA.remove(ChildOf(parentA));
        let removed = world.query(Removed(ChildOf));
        expect(removed).toHaveLength(1);
        expect(removed).toContain(childA);

        // Remove childB
        childB.remove(ChildOf(parentB));
        removed = world.query(Removed(ChildOf));
        expect(removed).toHaveLength(1);
        expect(removed).toContain(childB);
    });

    it('updateEach should work with Removed modifier for relations', () => {
        const Removed = createRemoved();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });

        const inventory = world.spawn();
        const gold = world.spawn();

        inventory.add(Contains(gold, { amount: 42 }));
        inventory.remove(Contains(gold));

        world.query(Removed(Contains), Contains(gold)).updateEach(([contains], entity) => {
            // Removed relation queries should still expose the removed pair's store data.
            expect(contains).toHaveProperty('amount', 42);
            // And its target
            expect(entity.targetFor(Contains)).toBe(gold);
        });
    });

    // @internal Tests internal implementation edge case with generation overflow
    it('[internal] should handle Changed modifier when trait registration causes generation overflow', () => {
        // Create a fresh world to control trait registration count
        const testWorld = createWorld();
        testWorld.init();

        // IsExcluded is already registered (bitflag=2 after), register 29 more to get bitflag=2^30
        const fillerTraits = Array.from({ length: 29 }, () => trait());
        for (const t of fillerTraits) {
            testWorld.spawn(t);
        }

        // Create Changed modifier - snapshots entityMasks with 1 generation
        const Changed = createChanged();

        // Spawn an entity so entityIndex.dense is not empty (required to trigger the bug)
        const entity = testWorld.spawn();

        // Register the 31st trait to trigger overflow (bitflag 2^30 -> 2^31 -> overflow)
        const Trait31 = trait();
        entity.add(Trait31);

        // Now entityMasks has 2 generations, but changedMask only has 1

        // Create the 32nd trait - will be in generation 1
        const NewTrait = trait();

        // This should not throw "cannot read properties of undefined (reading '0')"
        // when accessing changedMask[generationId][eid] where generationId is 1 but changedMask only has index 0
        expect(() => {
            testWorld.query(Changed(NewTrait));
        }).not.toThrow();
    });

Now let's understand the architecture deeper. I need to design the implementation.

Key concept: The tracking modifiers currently track at the trait bitmask level. Each `Trait` has a bitflag. For relation pairs, all targets share the
same underlying trait bitflag. To distinguish which pair changed, we need per-target tracking state.

Let me think about how to implement this cleanly.

### Approach

The requirement says "Tracking modifiers detect trait-level additions and removals but cannot distinguish which specific relation pair changed,
blocking per-target reactivity."

So the primary feature: `Added(Likes(alice))`, `Removed(Likes(alice))`, `Changed(Likes(alice))` should detect pair-level changes, and also
`Added(Likes('*'))` should act as wildcard (any pair).

We need to:

1. Extend `Modifier` to carry optional `relationPairs` (the pair filters), separate from `traits` (base traits). Actually the modifier currently
stores `traits` (base traits) and `traitIds`. We need to preserve the pairs. Let's add a `pairs` field to the modifier.

2. Factories `createAdded`, `createRemoved`, `createChanged` should accept `TraitOrRelation | RelationPair`, detect relation pairs and preserve them.
If a relation (not pair) is passed, it's trait-level as before (or wildcard?). The task says "Make tracking modifier factories accept new
`RelationPair`." So `Added(Relation)` should still work as trait-level tracking of the base trait. `Added(RelationPair)` should be pair-level.
`Added(RelationPair('*'))` wildcard pair-level.

3. In `createQueryInstance`, when processing a tracking modifier with pair filters:
   - The modifier's traits (base traits) get registered and bitmasks built as normal for trait-level matching.
   - Additionally, the query needs pair-level tracking. We need to track per-entity which specific pairs changed.
   - The query should include pair filters as "relationFilters" that are matched against actual pairs? But careful: for `Added(Likes(alice))`, the
query result should include entity if entity added Likes(alice). The static relation filter should check that entity has Likes(alice) currently (which
is normally true for an addition unless removed again). But for Removed, the pair is no longer present, so checking `hasRelationPair` would fail.
Existing `Removed(ChildOf)` works at trait level because `removeTraitFromEntity` marks dirty and checkTracking handles removed via dirty bit. For
pair-level removed, we need to track removed pair targets separately, because the pair is gone at query time.

Let me think of the cleanest design that fits the existing architecture.

#### Existing tracking architecture

- `world[$internal].trackingSnapshots: Map<id, generationMasks[]>`, where each generation mask is an array indexed by eid with trait bitmask (the
state at factory creation).
- `dirtyMasks: Map<id, ...>` - set when traits are added/removed. Used for Removed detection (removed-and-added-then-removed? Actually used to catch
removals where oldMask and currentMask are both 0).
- `changedMasks: Map<id, ...>` - set when changed() called.

- Each `QueryInstance.trackingGroups` has `bitmasks` (which traits the group tracks) and `trackers` (per-entity accumulated tracking state within the
observation window).

- When an add/remove/change event fires on a trait, `checkQueryTracking` is called for each tracking query containing that trait. It:
  - Checks static constraints (required/forbidden/or via entity masks)
  - Cross-event invalidation (remove invalidates add/change, add invalidates remove/change)
  - Accumulates the event bitflag in group.trackers
  - Verifies group satisfaction

- At query run, `runQuery` returns accumulated entities, then clears `entities` and resets trackers (`resetTrackingBitmasks`).

#### Pair tracking

We need a per-pair tracking mechanism. The challenge: pair events need to be recorded per (entity, target). The existing `trackers` are per entity
bitmasks, not per target.

Options:

**Option A: Keep pair event state in query-level structures keyed by entity+target.**

We can add to `QueryInstance` a `pairTrackers` structure: e.g., for each tracking group that has pair filters, a `Map<entityId, Map<targetId, {add,
remove, change}>>` or arrays. When pair events fire, `checkQueryTrackingWithRelations` (or a new function) updates this state.

Then at query population and run, entities that have any pair tracker matching the pair filters are added.

But the requirement "Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel" suggests we track both add and remove events per
pair and cancel them. This means for each (entity, target) we need to record pending add and remove flags.

Let me think about a simpler implementation that fits the existing incremental architecture.

Currently, `addTraitToEntity` fires `checkQueryTracking(world, query, entity, 'add', genId, bitflag)` for every tracking query that includes the
trait. `removeTraitFromEntity` fires `'remove'`. `setPairChanged` fires `'change'` with no target info passed to checkQueryTracking — it just passes
the bitflag.

To support pair-level:
- `addRelationPair` (in trait.ts) needs to fire pair-level add events for tracking queries, passing the target.
- `removeRelationPair`/`removeTrait`/`cleanupRelationTarget` needs to fire pair-level remove events with target.
- `setPairChanged` needs to fire pair-level change events with target.

We need a function like `checkQueryTrackingWithRelations` that takes a target and updates pair tracking state.

Let me design the data model:

```ts
type PairTracker = {
  // keyed by [entityId] -> Map<targetId, pairState>
  // pairState: { add: boolean, remove: boolean, change: boolean }
  // Actually we can encode with bits
};
```

Better: For each query, maintain `pairTrackers: Map<number /*eid*/, Map<number /*targetId*/, PairEventState>>`.

Actually we need to be careful about target `'*'` wildcard. For wildcard, any pair event counts. The pair filter's target can be `'*'` or a specific
entity id.

Let me define:

```
type PairEventState = { added: boolean; removed: boolean; changed: boolean };
```

Where `removed` is whether a pair was removed within the window (and not re-added), `added` whether added (and not removed), `changed` whether
changed. With opposite pair events canceling: add then remove → neither added nor removed. Remove then add → neither.

Now for a tracking group with pair filters, instead of just tracking bitflags per entity, we also need per-pair event state.

But there's a subtlety: query groups with multiple pair-tracked modifiers. E.g., `Added(Likes(alice), Fears(bob))` means AND: entity must have added
Likes(alice) AND added Fears(bob). `Or(Added(Likes(alice)), Added(Fears(bob)))` means OR. And `Added(Likes(alice))` combined with regular trait
`Position` in the same query.

The existing `TrackingGroup` groups by `(type, id, logic)`. Each group contains `traits` (from modifier.traits). For pair modifiers, the group would
have `pairs` (from modifier.pairs). A group might contain both traits and pairs.

Actually `createAdded` is called with `...inputs` where each input can be a Trait, Relation, or RelationPair. So `Added(Likes(alice), Position)`
creates a modifier with traits=[LikesTrait, Position] and pairs=[Likes(alice)]. This is an AND group: entity must have added both Likes(alice) AND
Position.

So each tracking group needs:
- `bitmasks` for trait-level tracking (as before)
- `pairs` for pair-level tracking: list of pairs.
- `pairTrackers` state per entity: which pairs have pending add/remove/change events.

For matching:
- For AND group: all traits must be tracked (via trackers) AND all pair filters must have matching pair events.
- For OR group: any trait tracked OR any pair event matches.

The existing `checkQueryTracking` handles trait trackers. We need a new function that also handles pair trackers, or extend it.

Let me think about how to integrate pair tracking into the group satisfaction check.

The cleanest: extend `TrackingGroup` with `pairs: RelationPair[]` and `pairTrackers: Map<number, Map<number, PairEventState>>` (or array-based).
Extend `checkQueryTracking` to:
1. On event, if `groupBitmask & eventBitflag` and group type matches, update trait trackers as before.
2. Additionally, if event is a pair event and group has pairs matching the target, update pairTrackers for that group.
3. When verifying group satisfaction, for each pair in group, check if pairTrackers for entity has a matching event state for that target.

But there's a complication: `checkQueryTracking` is called for all tracking queries on every trait add/remove/change, even those without pair info.
The event currently doesn't include target. We'll add an optional target parameter.

Now, "Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel." Suppose entity adds Likes(alice) (pair add event) then removes
Likes(alice) (pair remove event). The pair event state should end with `added=false, removed=false`. When the Added query runs, no match. When Removed
query runs, no match. But wait, should this also affect the trait-level trackers? If we also do trait-level tracking (because `Likes` trait was added
then removed), the trait-level `Removed(Likes)` would still match due to dirty bit. That's fine — pair-level and trait-level are distinct. For
`Added(Likes(alice))` specifically, pair state cancels.

Now, how do pair-level events interact with trait-level events? Let's consider `Added(Likes(alice))`:
- First pair added: trait `Likes` is added to entity → trait-level add event fires. Also pair add event for (entity, alice) fires.
- The query `Added(Likes(alice))` has group bitmask for Likes and pair filter for Likes(alice). It will get both events. The trait tracker accumulates
bitflag, and pair state gets added=true. Satisfaction requires both trait tracked and pair state matches. Good.
- Second pair added (Likes(bob)) when Likes already on entity: no trait-level add event (because `addTraitToEntity` returns early), but pair add event
fires. The trait tracker remains empty. For `Added(Likes(bob))`, pair state matches and that's enough (trait tracker doesn't need to be set? But the
AND group requires trait tracked). Hmm.

This is the key problem. If we require BOTH trait tracker AND pair tracker for AND group, then non-first pair additions would never match because
trait-level add doesn't fire. So the group satisfaction for a pair modifier should be based on pair tracking, not trait tracking, when the group
contains pairs? But the group may also contain regular traits in the same `Added` call: `Added(Likes(alice), Position)`. Then we need both: pair event
for Likes(alice) AND trait event for Position.

So the group satisfaction should be: for each trait in group.bitmasks that is a *regular* trait (not associated with a pair), require trait tracker;
for each pair in group.pairs, require pair event match. But what about the base trait of a pair — should it also require trait tracker? Let's think
about `Added(Likes(alice))`:
- If first pair added: trait Likes added. Pair state matches. If we don't require trait tracker, still matches. Good.
- If second pair added: trait not added. Pair state matches. Matches. Good.
- If `Added(Likes('*'))`: wildcard. Any pair add. Should match even non-first additions. Pair state for any target works.
- If `Added(Likes)` (relation, trait-level): should match only first pair addition (when trait added), as before. No pair filter → trait tracker
required. Good.

So the design: group satisfaction for a pair requires only pair tracker matching (not trait tracker for the underlying base trait). But what about
`Added(Likes(alice), Position)`? The group has pair Likes(alice) and trait Position. We require pair tracker for Likes(alice) AND trait tracker for
Position. Good.

But there's the issue of how `bitmasks`/`traits` relate to `pairs`. In `processTrackingModifier`, for each modifier, we add `modifier.traits` to
group.bitmasks. For a pair modifier, `modifier.traits` will include the base trait. If we require trait tracker for all traits in bitmasks, non-first
additions break. So we need to separate traits that are "pair-backed" from pure traits in the group.

Option: In `processTrackingModifier`, for pair modifiers, do NOT add the base trait to group.bitmasks (the tracking bitmask), only add to
query.traitInstances.all/static required so the static query constraint (has the relation trait) is applied? Wait, but for `Added(Likes(alice))`, do

So for pair tracking similarly, if entity adds Likes(alice) then removes it, the pair add event adds entity to query, then pair remove event cancels →
entity removed from Added query. That's the "cancel" requirement.

But note: existing behavior for trait-level `Added(Foo)` when Foo is removed and re-added: entityC removed then added again → matches because add
event fires again after remove. For pair: if alice removed then re-added, should match Added. So pair state must handle remove-then-add → added=true
again. The cancel rule: "opposite pair events on the same target cancel." If add then remove → canceled. If remove then add → canceled? Or re-added?
The phrase says opposite pair events on the same target cancel — I'd interpret symmetric: add+remove cancel (no add, no remove), remove+add cancel (no
remove, no add). But then if removed and re-added, should it match Added? Hmm. "Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target
cancel" — likely means any pair of opposite events on the same target cancel each other out, so if add→remove, both canceled (no Added, no Removed);

line 130-134). That's trait-level though. For pair-level, the requirement explicitly says opposite events cancel. I'll implement symmetric cancel:
each add increments a counter, each remove decrements; net add => added true; net remove => removed true; net zero => neither. Actually "cancel" with
counters: add then remove → net zero → canceled. Remove then add → net zero → canceled. Add, add, remove → net one add → added true. That's a robust
interpretation and also handles multiple adds of same target (which are no-ops anyway).

Let me now think about how to structure the code with minimal disruption.

#### Data structures

**Modifier**: add `pairs?: RelationPair[]` field (optional). `createModifier` can accept an optional pairs array. Or set after creation.

Actually let's look at how `createModifier` is used and typed. `Modifier<TTrait extends Trait[]>` has `traits: TTrait` and `traitIds`. We can add
`pairs?: RelationPair[]`.

**QueryInstance**: add `pairTrackers` maybe. But the state needs to live per tracking group. Let me extend `TrackingGroup`:

```ts
export type PairEventState = {
    added: number;   // net count of add events (could use boolean + counter)
    removed: number;
    changed: boolean;
};
```

Wait, for cancellation we need net counts. Let's use `added` and `removed` as counters (net), and `changed` as boolean. Actually add then remove:
added becomes 0 after increment then decrement... Let me use:
- `added: number` - increment on pair add, decrement on pair remove.
- `removed: number` - increment on pair remove, decrement on pair add.
- `changed: boolean` - set on change.

Then match for `add`: `state.added > 0`. Match for `remove`: `state.removed > 0`. This gives symmetric cancel. If add→remove: added 1→0, removed 0→1
(because remove decrements added and increments removed). Wait that's wrong: if add then remove, we want net zero, not removed=1. Let me define
counters carefully:

Use a single signed counter: `delta` where +1 = add, -1 = remove. Net:
- add then remove: +1 -1 = 0 → no add, no remove → canceled. ✓
- remove then add: -1 +1 = 0 → canceled. ✓
- add, add, remove: +1+1-1 = +1 → add. ✓
- remove, remove, add: -1-1+1 = -1 → remove. ✓

So use `delta: number`. Match add if delta > 0; match remove if delta < 0. And separate `changed: boolean` for changed events. For change events,
opposite events? Changed doesn't have an opposite except add/remove? The requirement only mentions opposite add/remove cancel. For change,
`Changed(Likes(alice))` should match if a change event fired for that pair. If pair is added then changed... changed state set. Fine.

But wait — should a `changed` event also set `delta`? No. Change tracking is separate.

Actually there's another subtlety: for `Added` with pair, should we also increment delta on add and decrement on remove regardless of the pair filter?
Yes, per target.

So:

```ts
type PairTrackerState = {
    delta: number;      // net add/remove count for this target
    changed: boolean;   // whether changed event fired for this target
};
```

`TrackingGroup`:
```ts
pairs: RelationPair[];
pairTrackers: Map<number, Map<number, PairTrackerState>>; // keyed by eid -> targetId -> state
```

Since entity IDs are numbers and target IDs are numbers, Map of Maps is fine, but performance-wise maybe array-based. Given this is a library with
performance concerns, but the task is correctness; I'll use Maps for clarity, maybe optimize later.

Actually, there's a complication: target `'*'` wildcard pair in a group. If group has pair `Likes('*')`, any pair event for Likes with any target
should count. So when a pair event fires with target t, for each pair in group:
- if pair.target === '*' → matches t
- if pair.target === t → matches
Then update the pairTracker under the actual target key `t` (so different targets produce distinct tracking). But for query matching with `'*'`, we
just need to know if any matching pair event happened. We can store per actual target.

For `Added(Likes('*'))`, entity added Likes(alice) → delta for alice = 1. At query population / event check, we check if any target in pairTrackers
has delta > 0 for a target matching the pair filter. Good.

#### Where to store pair state

Option: store in `TrackingGroup` as described. But `TrackingGroup` is in `query/types.ts` and used by `checkQueryTracking`. We'll need to modify
`checkQueryTracking` signature to accept target? Or create a new function `checkQueryTrackingWithPairs` that handles pair events separately.

Let me think about event flow. Currently:
- `addTraitToEntity` calls `query.checkTracking(world, entity, 'add', genId, bitflag)` or `checkQueryTrackingWithRelations(...)`.
- `removeTraitFromEntity` similar.
- `setPairChanged` calls `markChanged` which calls `query.checkTracking`/`checkQueryTrackingWithRelations`.

For pair-level events, we need to pass the target. I'll add an optional `target?: Entity` parameter to `checkQueryTracking` and
`checkQueryTrackingWithRelations`.

When `addRelationPair` fires (non-first addition or even first), we need to call the tracking update with `target`. Similarly

double-handle. Actually it's fine: the trait-level event updates trait trackers; the pair-level event (which we'll add after addRelationTarget)
updates pair trackers.

However, we need to make sure we don't fire pair events in cases where the pair wasn't actually added/removed (no-ops). `addRelationPair` returns
early if already has target. `removeRelationPair` handles no-op. Good.

#### Query matching with pair trackers

Now, when a pair event fires for entity+target, we need to find all tracking queries that:
1. have a tracking group with type matching eventType
2. that group has a pair filter matching this relation+target
3. static constraints pass
4. update pair trackers
5. check if entity matches → add/remove from query.

The existing flow calls `checkQueryTracking` from `addTraitToEntity` for queries in `instance.trackingQueries`. But a query is registered with a trait
instance's `trackingQueries` when the base trait is in `query.traitInstances.all`. So for `Added(Likes(alice))`, the Likes base trait is in the
query's all instances, so the query will be in `LikesInstance.trackingQueries`. When `addRelationPair` fires pair add for alice, we need to call
something that finds the query (it will iterate `instance.trackingQueries`), and within check, check if the group has a matching pair filter for the
target.

But there's a subtlety: the trait-level event (from `addTraitToEntity`) and pair-level event both iterate `instance.trackingQueries`. If we fire pair
event from within `addRelationPair` after `addTraitToEntity`, the query will be checked twice. That's okay as long as both update consistent state.
The trait event sets trait trackers; the pair event sets pair trackers. Both check satisfaction. Fine.

Actually, wait: do we even need to fire a separate pair-level event for the first pair addition, given the trait-level event already fires? We need
pair trackers to be set even for first additions (for the pair filter satisfaction). So yes, fire both.

But there's a risk: for `Added(Likes(alice))`, after trait-level event, satisfaction check happens. At that point pair tracker isn't set yet (pair
event fires after), so it won't add. Then pair event fires, satisfaction passes, adds. Good.

For `Removed(Likes(alice))` first/last removal:
- `removeTraitFromEntity` fires trait-level remove event → cross-event? For Removed group, trait tracker set. Then `removeRelationPair`/`removeTrait`
fires pair remove event → pair tracker set → satisfaction passes.
- But for `Removed(Likes(alice))` where removing non-last pair (entity still has Likes), only pair event fires (no trait-level remove). Pair tracker
handles it.

Now, what about static constraints in `checkQueryTracking`? For `Added(Likes(alice))` combined with `Position` (`world.query(Added(Likes(alice)),
Position)`), the static constraints include Position as required (because Position is a top-level trait parameter). So when pair event fires and we
check, entity must have Position. Good — "Pair modifiers combined with regular trait parameters in the same query must satisfy all constraints
together."

For `Added(Likes(alice))` alone, there are no static required traits (the base trait is in all, not required), so static passes. Good.

#### Population loop

When a query instance is created (first `world.query(...)` call), it needs to populate existing entities. The current population loop (lines 329-427)
checks tracking groups against snapshot/dirty/changed masks. For pair trackers, at query creation time, there may be existing pair events that
happened before the query was created (e.g., entity added Likes(alice) before `world.query(Added(Likes(alice)))` was called). Should that match?

after factory creation but before query creation, then when query is created, the population loop should find it via tracker state? Let's trace:

snapshot comparison. Good.

import { beforeEach, describe, expect, it } from 'vitest';
import { createWorld, relation, type TraitRecord, trait, universe } from '../src';

describe('World', () => {
    beforeEach(() => {
        universe.reset();
    });

    it('should create a world', () => {
        // World inits on creation.
        const world = createWorld();

        expect(world.isInitialized).toBe(true);
        expect(world.id).toBe(0);
        expect(universe.worlds[0]!).toBe(world);
        expect(universe.worldIndex.worldCursor).toBe(1);
    });

    it('should optionaly init lazily', () => {
        const world = createWorld({ lazy: true });
        expect(world.isInitialized).toBe(false);
        world.init();
        expect(world.isInitialized).toBe(true);
    });

    it('should reset the world', () => {
        const world = createWorld();
        world.reset();

        // Always has one entity that is the world itself.
        expect(world.entities.length).toBe(1);
    });

    it('reset should remove entities with auto-destroy relations', () => {
        const Node = trait();
        const ChildOf = relation({ autoDestroy: 'orphan', exclusive: true });

        const world = createWorld();

        // Create a parent node and a child node.
        const parentNode = world.spawn(Node);
        world.spawn(Node, ChildOf(parentNode));

        // Expect this to not throw, since the ChildOf relation will automatically
        // remove the child node when the parent node is destroyed first.
        expect(() => world.reset()).not.toThrow();

        // Always has one entity that is the world itself.
        expect(world.entities.length).toBe(1);
    });

    it('destroy should lead to entities with auto-destroy relations being removed as well', () => {
        const Node = trait();
        const ChildOf = relation({ autoDestroy: 'orphan', exclusive: true });

        const world = createWorld();

        // Create a parent node and two child nodes
        const parentNode = world.spawn(Node);
        world.spawn(Node, ChildOf(parentNode));
        world.spawn(Node, ChildOf(parentNode));

        // Expect this to not throw, since the ChildOf relation will automatically
        // remove the child node when the parent node is destroyed first
        expect(() => world.destroy()).not.toThrow();
    });

    it('errors if more than 16 worlds are created', () => {
        for (let i = 0; i < 16; i++) {
            createWorld();
        }

        expect(() => createWorld()).toThrow();
    });

    it('should recycle world IDs when destroyed', () => {
        const world = createWorld();
        const id = world.id;

        world.destroy();

        const newWorld = createWorld();
        expect(newWorld.id).toBe(id);
    });

    it('should add, remove and get singletons', () => {
        const Test = trait({ last: 0, delta: 0 });

        const world = createWorld(Test);
        expect(world.has(Test)).toBe(true);

        const { last: then, delta } = world.get(Test)!;
        expect(then).toBe(0);
        expect(delta).toBe(0);

        const Time = trait({ last: 0, delta: 0 });

        world.add(Time);
        expect(world.has(Time)).toBe(true);
        expect(world.has(Test)).toBe(true);

        // Does not show up in a query.
        const query = world.query(Time);
        expect(query.length).toBe(0);

        const time = world.get(Time)!;
        time.last = 1;
        time.delta = 1;
        world.set(Time, time);

        expect(time.last).toBe(1);
        expect(time.delta).toBe(1);

        world.remove(Time);
        expect(world.has(Time)).toBe(false);
    });

    it('should set singletons', () => {
        const Test = trait({ last: 0, delta: 0 });
        const world = createWorld(Test);

        world.set(Test, { last: 1, delta: 1 });

        expect(world.get(Test)!.last).toBe(1);
        expect(world.get(Test)!.delta).toBe(1);

        // Use callbacks to set.
        world.set(Test, (prev) => {
            return { last: prev.last + 1, delta: prev.delta + 1 };
        });

        expect(world.get(Test)!.last).toBe(2);
        expect(world.get(Test)!.delta).toBe(2);
    });

    it('should observe traits', () => {
        const TimeOfDay = trait({ hour: 0 });
        const world = createWorld(TimeOfDay);

        let timeOfDay: TraitRecord<typeof TimeOfDay> | undefined;
        world.onChange(TimeOfDay, (e) => {
            timeOfDay = e.get(TimeOfDay);
        });

        world.set(TimeOfDay, { hour: 1 });
        expect(timeOfDay).toEqual({ hour: 1 });
    });
});

Now, let me think about the type-level changes.

#### Type changes

`Modifier` type:
```ts
export type Modifier<TTrait extends Trait[] = Trait[], TType extends string = string> = {
    [$modifier]: true;
    type: TType;
    id: number;
    traits: TTrait;
    traitIds: number[];
    pairs?: RelationPair[];
};
```

Factories return a function accepting `...inputs: (TraitOrRelation | RelationPair)[]`. For each input:
- if relation pair → `pairs.push(input)`, `traits.push(input[$internal].relation[$internal].trait)`
- else if relation → traits.push(relation trait)
- else → traits.push(input)

Need `ExtractTraits` to handle RelationPair. Define:
```ts
export type TraitOrRelationOrPair = Trait | Relation<Trait> | RelationPair;
export type ExtractTrait<T> = T extends RelationPair<infer R> ? R : T extends Relation<infer R> ? R : T;
export type ExtractTraits<T extends TraitOrRelationOrPair[]> = { [K in keyof T]: ExtractTrait<T[K]> };
```

But wait — the `Modifier<TTrait>` generic currently carries the traits. For pair modifiers, the returned Modifier's `traits` are base traits. That's
fine for store/query result. But the modifier must also carry `pairs` to be used in query creation.

`OrParameter` should include RelationPair? The task says "Pair modifiers compose with Or." So `Or(Added(Likes(alice)))` — the nested modifier is
already a Modifier, so Or accepts Modifier. But `Or(Likes(alice), Position)`? Currently Or accepts `Trait | Modifier`. The task says pair modifiers
compose with Or — I think it means `Or(Added(Likes(alice)), Added(Fears(bob)))`, which works if Or accepts Modifier (already does). But maybe also
`Or(Likes(alice), Position)` should work? Not required. Let me focus on `Or(Added(pair), ...)`.

However, `Or` builds `ExtractTraitsFromOrParams` which filters out modifiers; fine. But `Or` with relation pairs as direct traits? The task only says
"Pair modifiers compose with Or", so I'll make Or accept nested tracking modifiers (already does). But there's an issue: in `createQueryInstance`, for
Or modifier, nested tracking modifiers are processed with `processTrackingModifier(..., 'or', ...)`. If the nested modifier has pairs,
`processTrackingModifier` must handle pairs.

Also `OrParameter` currently `Trait | Modifier`. A RelationPair is neither Trait nor Modifier. If someone does `Or(Added(Likes(alice)),
Added(Fears(bob)))`, params are Modifiers, fine. If someone does `Or(Likes(alice), Position)`, currently RelationPair isn't allowed type-wise. Not
required by task, but "Pair modifiers compose with Or" could mean `Or(Added(pair))` which is already allowed type-wise. I'll keep OrParameter as is
but maybe extend to include RelationPair for completeness? Let's check `isModifier` checks in Or — a RelationPair isn't a modifier and not a Trait, so
it'd be pushed as `traits.push(param as Trait)` which is wrong. To avoid breaking, I'll leave OrParameter as `Trait | Modifier` but the nested
modifier route works.

Actually wait, let me reconsider: in `Or` implementation:
```ts
for (const param of params) {
    if ((param as Modifier)[$modifier]) modifiers.push(param as Modifier);
    else traits.push(param as Trait);
}
```
If a RelationPair is passed to Or, it would be pushed as a Trait. That's a bug if we extend the type. Since not required, I'll leave it but perhaps
add support anyway? Better to support `Or(Likes(alice), Position)` too? The requirement only mentions "Pair modifiers compose with Or", so I'll
support nested pair modifiers (already works once modifiers carry pairs) and not necessarily direct relation pairs in Or. But to be safe and
consistent, I might extend Or to handle RelationPair by pushing it into traits? No—Or's `traits` are used for bitmasks and need Trait instances. Let
me keep it minimal.

Now, the hash. `createQueryHash` currently:
- for relation pair param: encodes `relationId * 10000000 + targetId + 5000000`.
- for modifier param: for each traitId, `modifierId * 100000 + traitId`.
- for trait: traitId.

For pair modifiers, the hash needs to encode the pair target too, so different pair targets produce distinct cached queries. We need to add pair info
to modifier hash. E.g., for each pair in modifier, encode `modifierId * 100000 + traitId` AND a separate encoded pair value. Let's design a stable
encoding. We need to ensure `Added(Likes(alice))` vs `Added(Likes(bob))` produce different hashes. Also `Added(Likes(alice))` vs `Added(Likes('*'))`
differ.

The existing encoding `relationId * 10000000 + targetId + 5000000` for a relation pair param. We can reuse for pairs in modifiers. For wildcard,
targetId = -1 → value = relationId*10000000 + 4999999. Good.

Let me modify `createQueryHash` for modifier params:
```ts
const modifierId = param.id;
const traitIds = param.traitIds;
for (traitId of traitIds) sortedIDs[cursor++] = modifierId * 100000 + traitId;
const pairs = param.pairs;
if (pairs) for (pair of pairs) {
    const relationTraitId = pair[$internal].relation[$internal].trait.id;
    const target = pair[$internal].target;
    const targetId = typeof target === 'number' ? target : -1;
    sortedIDs[cursor++] = modifierId * 100000000 + relationTraitId * 100000 + targetId + 5000000;
}
```

#### createModifier

`createModifier(type, id, traits)` — I'll add a fourth optional param `pairs`.

```ts
export function createModifier<TTrait extends Trait[] = Trait[], TType extends string = string>(
    type: TType,
    id: number,
    traits: TTrait,
    pairs?: RelationPair[]
): Modifier<TTrait, TType> {
    return {
        [$modifier]: true,
        type,
        id,
        traits,
        traitIds: traits.map(t => t.id),
        ...(pairs && pairs.length ? { pairs } : {}),
    } as const;
}
```

Factories:
```ts
return <T extends TraitOrRelationOrPair[]>(...inputs: T): Modifier<ExtractTraits<T>, `added-${number}`> => {
    const traits: Trait[] = [];
    const pairs: RelationPair[] = [];
    for (const input of inputs) {
        if (isRelationPair(input)) {
            pairs.push(input);
            traits.push(input[$internal].relation[$internal].trait);
        } else if (isRelation(input)) {
            traits.push(input[$internal].trait);
        } else {
            traits.push(input);
        }
    }
    return createModifier(`added-${id}`, id, traits as ExtractTraits<T>, pairs);
};
```

Let me rewrite `checkQueryTracking`'s satisfaction loop:

```ts
for (let i = 0; i < trackingGroupsLen; i++) {
    const group = trackingGroups[i];
    const groupType = group.type;
    const groupLogic = group.logic;
    const groupBitmasks = group.bitmasks;
    const groupBitmask = groupBitmasks[eventGenerationId];
    const groupTrackers = group.trackers;
    const groupPairs = group.pairs;

    // 1. Handle event (cross-invalidation + tracker/pair updates)
    if (groupBitmask && (groupBitmask & eventBitflag)) {
        // cross-event invalidation
        ...
        if (groupType === eventType) {
            ... update trait trackers
        }
    }

    // Update pair trackers (independent of trait bitmask? pair event has bitflag from base trait, so groupBitmask includes base trait bit)
    if (target !== undefined && groupPairs.length && groupType === eventType) {
        // update pair state for matching pairs
    }

    // 2. Verify satisfaction
    let traitMatched = groupLogic === 'and'; // AND starts true, OR starts false
    // for traits
    if (groupLogic === 'or') {
        hasOrGroup = true;
        // check trait trackers for any match
        if (!traitMatched) {
            for each gen/mask: if tracker & mask { traitMatched = true; break; }
        }
    } else {
        // AND: all traits must be tracked
        for each gen/mask: if (tracker & mask) !== mask { traitMatched = false; break; }
    }

    // pairs
    let pairMatched = false;
    if (groupPairs.length) {
        const targetMap = group.pairTrackers.get(eid);
        if (groupLogic === 'and') {
            pairMatched = true;
            for (const pair of groupPairs) {
                // need a matching event for this pair
                if (!hasMatchingPairEvent(targetMap, pair, eventType)) { pairMatched = false; break; }
            }
        } else {
            // OR: any pair matches
            for (const pair of groupPairs) {
                if (hasMatchingPairEvent(targetMap, pair, eventType)) { pairMatched = true; break; }
            }
        }
    } else {
        pairMatched = groupLogic === 'and'; // no pairs: AND true, OR false
    }

    const matched = groupLogic === 'and' ? (traitMatched && pairMatched) : (traitMatched || pairMatched);

    if (groupLogic === 'or') {
        if (matched) anyOrMatched = true;
    } else {
        if (!matched) return false;
    }
}
```

```ts
function hasMatchingPairEvent(targetMap, pair, eventType): boolean {

        if (eventType === 'add' && state.delta > 0) return true;
        if (eventType === 'remove' && state.delta < 0) return true;
```

Let me write helper:
```ts
function updatePairTracker(group, eid, relationTraitId, targetId, eventType) {
    let relationMap = group.pairTrackers.get(eid);
    if (!relationMap) { relationMap = new Map(); group.pairTrackers.set(eid, relationMap); }
    let targetMap = relationMap.get(relationTraitId);
    if (!targetMap) { targetMap = new Map(); relationMap.set(relationTraitId, targetMap); }
    let state = targetMap.get(targetId);
    if (!state) { state = { delta: 0, changed: false }; targetMap.set(targetId, state); }
    if (eventType === 'add') state.delta++;
    else if (eventType === 'remove') state.delta--;
    else if (eventType === 'change') state.changed = true;
}
```

For update, we need to know which pairs in the group match this event. We can iterate group.pairs and for each, get the base trait's instance; if
generationId/bitflag match and pair target matches actual target, update. But note multiple pairs in same group could share same relation trait (e.g.,
`Added(Likes(alice), Likes(bob))`) — that would add both pairs, but an event for alice should only update alice's state. The condition `pairTarget ===
'*' || pairTarget === targetId` handles that.

But what if the group has wildcard `Likes('*')` and also specific `Likes(alice)`? Both in same group. An add of alice would match both and update
state twice (delta++ twice). That's wrong. We should only update once per (relationTraitId, targetId). Let me update per relation+target once,
regardless of how many pairs match. So in the update phase, compute the set of (relationTraitId, targetId) to update from matching pairs, dedupe. Or
simpler: check if any pair in group matches this relation+target, then update once.


Now, the group satisfaction `hasMatchingPairEvent`:
```ts
function hasMatchingPairEvent(group, eid, pair, eventType): boolean {
    const relationMap = group.pairTrackers.get(eid);
    if (!relationMap) return false;
    const pairTrait = pair[$internal].relation[$internal].trait;
    const targetMap = relationMap.get(pairTrait.id);
    if (!targetMap) return false;
    const pairTarget = pair[$internal].target;
    if (pairTarget === '*') {
        for (const state of targetMap.values()) {
            if (eventType === 'add' && state.delta > 0) return true;
            if (eventType === 'remove' && state.delta < 0) return true;
            if (eventType === 'change' && state.changed) return true;
        }
        return false;
    }
    const state = targetMap.get(pairTarget);
    if (!state) return false;
    return (eventType === 'add' && state.delta > 0) ||
           (eventType === 'remove' && state.delta < 0) ||
           (eventType === 'change' && state.changed);
}
```

the same? In createTrait, `id = traitId++`; `Trait[$internal].id = id` and public `Trait.id` = id. So same. Use `pairTrait.id`.

But note: the trait instance's `generationId`/`bitflag` are world-specific. In the update phase we use
`getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, pairTrait)` and compare to eventGenerationId/bitflag. That's correct.

```ts
export function logPairEvent(world: World, relationTrait: Trait, entity: Entity, target: Entity, eventType: EventType) {
    const ctx = world[$internal];
    const eid = getEntityId(entity);
    const targetId = getEntityId(target);
    for (const [id, events] of ctx.pairTrackingEvents) {
        let eidMap = events.get(eid);
        if (!eidMap) { eidMap = new Map(); events.set(eid, eidMap); }
        let targetMap = eidMap.get(relationTrait.id);
        if (!targetMap) { targetMap = new Map(); eidMap.set(relationTrait.id, targetMap); }
        let state = targetMap.get(targetId);
        if (!state) { state = { delta: 0, changed: false }; targetMap.set(targetId, state); }
        if (eventType === 'add') state.delta++;
        else if (eventType === 'remove') state.delta--;
        else if (eventType === 'change') state.changed = true;
    }
}
```

Hmm, but this logs for ALL tracking ids. Should it log only ids whose factories were created? Yes, `pairTrackingEvents` map only has entries for ids
that had `setTrackingMasks` called. Good.

Let me restructure: Create a shared helper:

```ts
export function notifyTrackingQueries(
    world: World,
    query: QueryInstance,
    entity: Entity,
    eventType: EventType,
    generationId: number,
    bitflag: number,
    target?: Entity
) {
    const match =
        query.relationFilters && query.relationFilters.length > 0
            ? checkQueryTrackingWithRelations(world, query, entity, eventType, generationId, bitflag, target)
            : query.checkTracking(world, entity, eventType, generationId, bitflag, target);
    if (match) query.add(entity);
    else query.remove(world, entity);
}
```

Actually the existing code duplicates this. Let me just modify `query.checkTracking` signature to accept optional target and modify the call sites to
pass it.
